Of particular interest is the optimal average probability of success, $P^{Q,\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{opt}}_{n,m}$, which is numerically conjectured to satisfy the bound $P^{Q,\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{opt}}_{n,m}\leq \frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{m}{n}}$. Recent constructions of $(n,n-1)$ QRACs by Suzuki and $(n,n-2)$ QRACs by Akibue et al. meet this bound exactly, raising the question of their strict optimality. In this work, we settle this question by proving the conjectured upper bound for $m\in\{n-1,n-2\}$, thereby precisely determining $P^{Q,\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{opt}}_{n,n-1}$ and $P^{Q,\mathrm{avg},\mathrm{opt}}_{n,n-2}$. The proof utilizes a translation recently studied by Lin and de Wolf from local to global reconstruction via pretty good measurement, along with dimensional and positive-semidefinite constraints on an induced channel.

</details>


### [31] [Quantum algorithm for Clifford multiplication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10473)
*Kagwe A. Muchane*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Quantum computers can perform the geometric product of dense multivectors in Clifford algebra much faster than classical computers, highlighting its potential for quantum algorithms and simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The traditional computation of geometric products in Clifford algebra is slow for dense multivectors, prompting exploration of faster quantum approaches.

Method: Utilizing amplitude encoding on a quantum computer, the geometric product is computed in O(polylog N) time while using logarithmic space and sublogarithmic circuit depth.

Result: This approach demonstrates an exponential speedup compared to classical algorithms, which require O(N^{ω/2}) operations.

Conclusion: Establishing Clifford multiplication as a quantum primitive opens avenues for efficient quantum geometric algorithms and simulations.

Abstract: Given two dense multivectors of the Clifford algebra $C\ell(V, Q)$ with $N=2^{p+q}$ coefficients, the fastest known classical algorithms compute their geometric product in $O(N^{ω/2})$ arithmetic operations, where $ω$ denotes the matrix multiplication exponent. I show that, under amplitude encoding, a quantum computer executes the geometric product in $O(\operatorname{polylog} N)$ time, using logarithmic space with sublogarithmic circuit depth. This exponential speedup establishes Clifford multiplication as a quantum primitive, providing an efficient computational foundation for quantum geometric algorithms and relativistic simulations.

</details>


### [32] [The General Quantum Limit for and the Optimization of the Minimum Measurable Frequency Shift in a Laser](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10518)
*Selim M. Shahriar,Ruoxi Zhu,Jinyang Li,Zifan Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper reveals that the minimum measurable frequency shift for a single-mode ideal laser is influenced by phase diffusion and vacuum shot noise, impacting practical sensors. It provides optimal MMFS values for three sensing modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reassess the conventional understanding of minimum measurable frequency shifts in laser applications, particularly for sensor design.

Method: Analyzing the influences of phase diffusion and shot noise on MMFS and calculating optimal values for three sensor types: Mach-Zehnder Interferometer, passive Fabry-Perot cavity, and heterodyning.

Result: Identified that MMFS is the geometric mean of measurement bandwidth and Schwalow-Townes linewidth, affected by factors that can exceed unity, along with determining optimal MMFS values for specific sensing modalities.

Conclusion: Optimal MMFS values are achievable for the specified sensor modalities under certain conditions, challenging prior assumptions in laser frequency shift measurement.

Abstract: We show that, contrary to conventional understanding, the minimum measurable frequency shift (MMFS) for a single-mode ideal laser is determined by a combination of phase diffusion caused by spontaneous emission and the shot noise caused by the vacuum mode. For all practical sensors, the MMFS is found to be given by the geometric mean of the measurement bandwidth and the Schwalow-Townes Linewidth, multiplied by a factor which can be much larger than unity under certain conditions. We determine the optimal values of the MMFS for three different sensing modalities, an unbalanced Mach-Zehnder Interferometer, a passive Fabry-Perot cavity (FPC), and heterodyning with a reference laser, and identify the conditions needed for reaching these values of the MMFS.

</details>


### [33] [Coherent Quantum Schrodinger Bridge: Two-Boundary Optimal Control for Quantum Algorithm Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10550)
*Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a coherent Quantum Schrödinger Bridge (QSB) framework for quantum algorithms, emphasizing optimal control via weak values and unifying various algorithms into a geometric principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the two-boundary nature of quantum algorithms and propose a unified framework for optimal control in quantum computing.

Method: The framework formulates a coherent Quantum Schrödinger Bridge (QSB) using Pontryagin's principle to derive a universal optimal Hamiltonian based on weak values, applied to various algorithms.

Result: The study reconstructs Grover's algorithm, the quantum Fourier transform, and quantum singular value transformation, linking their components to the optimal weak-value drift.

Conclusion: Algorithm design in quantum computing can be viewed as selecting computational boundary conditions to realize optimal flow, unifying distinct quantum algorithm paradigms.

Abstract: Quantum algorithms are intrinsically two-boundary processes: an input state is prepared, and an output state or subspace is selected as the computational answer. We formulate this observation as a coherent Quantum Schrödinger Bridge (QSB), a pure-state Hamiltonian counterpart of Schrödinger bridge theory in which the endpoint constraint is imposed on state vectors and the transport cost is the quadratic control action. In this setting Aharonov's two-state vector becomes the natural optimal-control pair: a forward state from the input and a backward state from the target. Pontryagin's principle then yields a universal optimal Hamiltonian whose weak value is purely imaginary in the geodesic gauge. Thus weak values are not an auxiliary interpretation; they are the local response functions that quantify the drift of the pre-selected state toward the post-selected boundary. Applying this framework to unstructured search, periodicity finding, and matrix arithmetic, we reconstruct Grover's algorithm, the quantum Fourier transform underlying Shor's algorithm, and quantum singular value transformation (QSVT). The usual circuit components -- oracles, diffusion reflections, controlled phases, and signal-processing rotations -- emerge as Lie-algebraic syntheses of the optimal weak-value drift. This perspective unifies distinct algorithmic paradigms into a single geometric principle: algorithm design is the problem of choosing computational boundary conditions and realizing the corresponding optimal flow.

</details>


### [34] [The Virtuous Cycle of Quantum-Classical Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10563)
*Nenad Tomašev,Jarrod R. McClean,Johannes Bausch*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper discusses the interplay between artificial intelligence and quantum computing, highlighting benefits of machine learning in quantum contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how advancements in artificial intelligence and quantum computing can accelerate scientific discovery and each other's progress.

Method: A review of opportunities in quantum computing for machine learning, emphasizing the integration of both fields.

Result: Identification of key areas where quantum computing can enhance machine learning applications, and vice versa.

Conclusion: The relationship between these two domains can foster a mutually beneficial cycle of innovation.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence and quantum computing have both seen tremendous progress in recent years, opening up new avenues for accelerating scientific discovery. Classical machine learning has already proven to be useful for addressing challenges in quantum computation; and hardware progress is well underway towards the early fault-tolerant regime. On the other hand, the emerging field of quantum machine learning is aimed at utilizing the strengths of both computational paradigms, via the development of learning algorithms or models that benefit from running on quantum devices or from training on quantum data. We therefore argue that both of these paradigms stand to benefit substantially from each other. Here we review the most salient opportunities for machine learning in quantum computing, hoping to inspire a virtuous cycle through which both fields can mutually benefit.

</details>


### [35] [Semiclassical asymptotics of multiphotonic scattering probabilities with partial indistinguishability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10632)
*Alonso Botero,Hubert de Guise,Orlando Melo-Vargas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A framework for calculating multiphotonic scattering probabilities in lossless multiport interferometers is proposed, emphasizing asymptotic behavior and classical features in partially indistinguishable photons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To compute scattering probabilities in advanced quantum systems with arbitrary photon numbers and indistinguishability, addressing limitations in existing methodologies.

Method: Utilizes a toroidal expansion of multiphotonic states, mapping relative phases to a probability simplex to analyze asymptotic behaviors and scattering probabilities.

Result: Establishes a new asymptotic formula for multiphotonic probabilities, providing insights into classical measures and enabling predictions about photon bunching patterns.

Conclusion: The framework offers a novel approach for understanding photon distributions, linking classical aspects of the map to quantum behavior, and yielding testable outcomes in experimental setups.

Abstract: We propose a framework for computing multiphotonic scattering probabilities in a lossless multiport interferometer for arbitrary photon numbers and degrees of indistinguishability. By exploiting a toroidal expansion of multiphotonic states in tensor powers of single-particle states, the framework defines a map from a torus of relative phases to the probability simplex that governs the asymptotic behavior of scattering probabilities in the large-photon limit. Specifically, the probabilities concentrate on the "classically allowed region" defined by the map, and the slowly-varying part of the multiphotonic distribution reproduces a classical measure induced by the map. As a result, we are able to establish a new asymptotic formula for the multiphotonic probabilities in a general scenario of partially indistinguishable photons, while also providing a single-particle picture to explain the asymptotics of known multiphotonic transition amplitudes in the fully indistinguishable case. More broadly, our framework yields new, directly testable consequences in relation to asymptotic photon bunching patterns: it translates features of the classical map -- such as caustics and voids -- into direct predictions about regions of large or exponentially suppressed photon-distribution probability.

</details>


### [36] [Learning Topological Quantum Phases from Limited Subsystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10656)
*Mehran Khosrojerdi,Sougato Bose,Alessandro Cuccoli,Paola Verrucchi,Abolfazl Bayat,Leonardo Banchi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a supervised learning method to classify quantum phases using small subsystems, avoiding the need for full system measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterization of quantum topological phases is typically limited by the need for full-system measurements, which are often impractical in experiments.

Method: The framework uses a quantum kernel derived from the reduced density matrices of small subsystems to classify quantum phases efficiently.

Result: The method successfully classifies phases of two one-dimensional spin models with high accuracy using only one to four sites, and it generalizes to larger systems.

Conclusion: Local reduced density matrices retain essential information about global topological phases, enabling effective characterization of complex quantum many-body phase diagrams.

Abstract: Characterizing quantum topological phases requires measuring non-local string order parameters, demanding access to the full system, which is often experimentally unfeasible. In this work, we introduce a data-efficient supervised learning framework that circumvents this limitation by recognizing quantum phases from small subsystems. Our protocol utilizes a quantum kernel constructed from the reduced density matrices of these subsystems, which can be efficiently estimated experimentally. We benchmark our framework with the classification of the phase diagrams of two spin models on one-dimensional lattices, namely the generalized cluster-Ising spin-1/2 chain and the anisotropic Haldane spin-1 chain. Remarkably, our approach achieves high accuracy in phase classification when operations are limited to as few as one to four sites, and it also generalizes to longer chains even when trained on moderate system sizes. These findings demonstrate that local reduced density matrices preserve vital signatures of global topological phases, offering a practical route to characterize rich phase diagrams of quantum many-body systems.

</details>


### [37] [Security of passive entanglement-based key distribution protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10659)
*Shun Kawakami,Koichi Takasugi,Koji Azuma*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proves the security of passive entanglement-based key distribution protocols, specifically BBM92 and QCKA, in the asymptotic regime and shows that their key generation rates are similar to active protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the security challenges posed by passive measurement setups in entanglement-based key distribution protocols, especially with biased basis choices, where standard security proofs are inadequate.

Method: We establish a comprehensive security proof for passive BBM92 and extend it to QCKA with multiple parties, focusing on the asymptotic regime.

Result: The security of passive BBM92 with biased basis choice is validated, and the key generation rate is shown to be nearly identical to that of active protocols.

Conclusion: The findings support the practical use of passive implementations of entanglement-based key distribution protocols by providing a theoretical security framework.

Abstract: Entanglement-based key distribution protocols, such as the Bennett-Brassard-Mermin 1992 (BBM92) protocol and quantum conference key agreement (QCKA), are promising applications of quantum networks. In practical implementations, passive measurement setups are widely adopted because of their simplicity. However, the security analysis of passive protocols with biased basis choice is highly nontrivial, since standard proof techniques for threshold detectors are generally not applicable in this setting. In this work, we establish the security of passive entanglement-based key distribution protocols in the asymptotic regime. Specifically, we prove the security of passive BBM92 with biased basis choice and extend the proof to passive QCKA with an arbitrary number of parties. In addition, we numerically show that the key generation rate of passive BBM92 is almost identical to that of the corresponding active protocol. Our results provide a theoretical foundation for practical passive implementations of entanglement-based key distribution protocols.

</details>


### [38] [Passive spectral-admittance bounds and exact continuum certificates for multiresonator quantum-memory interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10704)
*Maxim V. Churilov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a detailed model for assessing broadband quantum-memory interfaces using a passive multiresonator design. It establishes conditions for write probabilities and efficiencies, provides a computable certification method, and exemplifies its application with a specific design, demonstrating high write guarantees without claiming overall optimality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a continuous-band certificate for quantum-memory interfaces, overcoming limitations of previous methods that did not guarantee reversible storage or operational efficiency.

Method: The paper models a passive one-port multiresonator interface using positive-real spectral admittance, mapping the one-photon subspace into long-lived registers, and establishes write probabilities and worst-case efficiencies. It uses minimax synthesis and Sturm root counting for computable certificates.

Result: An 11-mode design demonstrates a bounded reflection where the minimal reflection is between 0.064112405 and 0.0641125. This implies a uniform write guarantee of over 0.995889587.

Conclusion: The findings provide a reproducible certification for the interface designed, focusing on specific operational parameters without asserting comprehensive optimality across all potential configurations.

Abstract: Broadband quantum-memory interfaces are often assessed by center-frequency impedance matching or by a sampled efficiency curve. Neither supplies an operational continuous-band certificate, and absorption is not automatically reversible storage. We model a passive one-port multiresonator interface by a positive-real spectral admittance with explicitly identified controlled output channels. If their one-photon subspace is mapped isometrically into long-lived registers, the write probability for a normalized spectrum $f$ supported in a band $\mathcal{B}$ is $1-\int_{\mathcal{B}} |r(iω)|^2 |f(ω)|^2\,dω$, and the worst-case write efficiency is $1-\|r\|_{L^\infty(\mathcal{B})}^2$. We prove that a finite passive rational interface cannot have zero reflection on a nonzero interval and derive the Bode--Fano floor $\|r\|_\infty \geq \exp[-πκ/(2B)]$ for a band of half-width $B$. At fixed pole locations, minimax synthesis is a quasiconvex semi-infinite problem in the oscillator strengths. We then give an exact computer-assisted certificate: after a decimal design is converted into an explicit rational system, the continuum reflection bound becomes positivity of one univariate polynomial and is proved by Sturm root counting; exact Routh--Hurwitz determinants certify stability and minimum phase. In units $κ=2$ and $B=1$, an 11-mode design obeys $0.064112405 \leq \|r\|_\infty < 0.0641125$, implying a conditional uniform write guarantee above $0.995889587$. This is a reproducible certificate for a specified interface, not a claim of global movable-pole optimality or of an experimentally complete memory.

</details>


### [39] [MDQEC-QAS: Meta-Decoding for Quantum Error Correction with Hardware-Aware VQC Search and Confidence-Gated Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10707)
*Prashant Kumar Choudhary,Nouhaila Innan,Muhammad Shafique,Rajeev Singh*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified meta-decoding framework for quantum error correction capable of learning across various codes and noise types, highlighting the efficacy of confidence-aware selective recovery over traditional decoders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To streamline quantum error correction by creating a single framework that adapts across multiple stabilizer codes and noise environments, thereby improving efficiency and performance.

Method: A meta-decoding framework is established to learn syndrome-to-recovery mappings, incorporating classical Meta-MLP and variational quantum circuit (VQC) approaches while employing hardware-aware architecture search.

Result: Meta-MLP achieves high teacher-label accuracies across multiple regimes, but logical evaluation shows limitations in the Planar5x5 setup. Confidence-gated fallback improves logical-failure ratios significantly for both methods.

Conclusion: Confidence-aware selective recovery outperforms unconditional teacher replacement, emphasizing the need for adaptive strategies in quantum error correction.

Abstract: We propose a unified meta-decoding framework for quantum error correction that learns syndrome-to-recovery mappings across multiple stabilizer codes and noise settings, without requiring separate decoders for each configuration. The benchmark includes FiveQubit, Steane, Planar3x3, and Planar5x5 codes, four noise families, and five evaluation regimes: interpolation, unseen-p transfer, unseen-noise transfer, few-shot unseen-code adaptation, and few-shot held-out-size adaptation. We compare a classical Meta-MLP teacher-trained baseline with variational quantum circuit (VQC) meta-decoders selected through hardware-aware quantum architecture search over qubit count, circuit depth, and entangling topology. The Meta-MLP achieves teacher-label accuracies of 0.9993, 0.9118, 0.9342, 0.6304, and 0.7548 across the five regimes, while the hardware-aware VQC achieves 0.9400, 0.8495, 0.8415, 0.5678, and 0.7143. However, logical-level evaluation shows that high teacher-label accuracy alone is insufficient in the most challenging Planar5x5 setting. During interpolation, the raw logical-failure ratios relative to the teacher are 12.08 and 25.91 for the Meta-MLP and VQC, respectively, whereas confidence-gated fallback reduces them to 1.71 and 1.11. These results support confidence-aware selective recovery rather than unconditional teacher replacement.

</details>


### [40] [Nonlocal nonstabilizerness for slightly entangled quantum many-body states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10714)
*Lei-Yi-Nan Liu,Jian Cui*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a new framework for evaluating nonlocal nonstabilizerness in bipartite entanglement using a Schmidt-reference-state approach, simplifying the optimization problem and enabling efficient analysis of weakly entangled many-body states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating nonlocal nonstabilizerness is complex due to nonconvex optimization over local unitary transformations, which this study aims to address.

Method: The authors propose a Schmidt-reference-state framework that directly constructs the evaluation from the sorted Schmidt spectrum, conjecturing a relationship with nonlocal stabilizer Rényi entropy (SRE).

Result: Analytical and numerical evidence supports the conjecture that nonlocal SRE corresponds to the SRE of the reference state; applications on various states reveal insights into the entanglement spectrum.

Conclusion: The framework enhances the accessibility of nonlocal nonstabilizerness for many-body systems, suggesting entanglement spectra as a valuable tool for investigating nonlocal magic resources in quantum systems.

Abstract: Nonlocal nonstabilizerness quantifies the irreducible magic resource encoded in bipartite entanglement, but its evaluation is generally hindered by a highly nonconvex optimization over local unitary transformations. Here we propose a Schmidt-reference-state framework that replaces this optimization by a direct construction from the sorted Schmidt spectrum. We conjecture that the nonlocal stabilizer Rényi entropy (SRE) is given by the SRE of the corresponding reference state, and support this conjecture through analytical and numerical evidences. Our framework makes nonlocal nonstabilizerness efficiently accessible for weakly entangled many-body states whenever the entanglement spectrum is available. Applying it to Haar-random states, critical spin chains, and PXP dynamics, we show that nonlocal SRE captures nonstabilizer structures in the entanglement spectrum that are invisible to conventional entanglement measures. Our results establish entanglement spectra as a powerful window into irreducible nonstabilizer correlations, opening a broadly applicable route to studying nonlocal magic resources of quantum many-body systems in and out of equilibrium.

</details>


### [41] [Connectivity-induced surface-loss penalty in superconducting qubit-coupler lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10743)
*Xu-Yang Gu,Gui-Han Liang,Ming-Chuan Wang,Yongxi Xiao,Cheng-Lin Deng,Zheng-He Liu,Tian-Ming Li,Kai Xu,Zhongcheng Xiang,Heng Fan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Investigates the increased surface loss in multiqubit superconducting transmon qubits due to connectivity in coupler lattices, with insights for design optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why isolated qubits exhibit longer lifetimes compared to those in multiqubit processors.

Method: Finite-element simulations were performed to analyze surface participation ratios and dielectric loss in qubits within a flip-chip qubit-coupler lattice.

Result: Higher connectivity between qubits and couplers significantly increases surface loss, with factors of 1.3 and 1.8 noted for two and four couplers, respectively.

Conclusion: Optimizing geometric designs for qubit electrodes and coupling claws can mitigate connectivity-induced surface loss in multiqubit processors.

Abstract: Recent advances in design and fabrication have increased the energy-relaxation times of isolated superconducting transmon qubits to the hundreds-of-microseconds regime, with reported values exceeding 500 $μ$s. However, the same progress has not automatically translated to multiqubit processors, where qubits are embedded in connected qubit-coupler lattices and often exhibit much shorter lifetimes than isolated qubits. To identify possible sources of this discrepancy, here we use finite-element simulation to investigate how surface participation ratios and the resulting surface dielectric loss change when a qubit is embedded in a flip-chip qubit-coupler lattice. Controlled comparisons show that higher connectivity can indeed lead to larger surface loss: in the simulated lattice, connecting a qubit to two and four couplers increases the surface loss by factors of 1.3 and 1.8, respectively. We attribute this change to the combined effects of added edge fields from coupling claws, field redistribution over the larger connected metal network, and hybridization with coupler modes. We further examine how this connectivity-induced surface-loss penalty depends on the geometric design parameters of both the qubit electrodes and the coupling claws, and derive guidelines for designing low-loss multiqubit processors.

</details>


### [42] [Spectral gap of Lee-Yang Hamiltonians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10765)
*Chaithanya Rayudu,Jun Takahashi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper establishes a lower bound on the spectral gap of ground states for Hamiltonians under certain conditions, contributing to quantum computation by offering an efficient algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the implications of the Lee-Yang theorem and its quantum extensions on the spectral properties of Hamiltonians and their computational applications.

Method: The authors leverage the zero-freeness of the partition function to derive properties of imaginary-time correlations, leading to a lower bound on the spectral gap.

Result: Under a uniform Z-field, the ground state exhibits a spectral gap of at least h/4, independent of system size and coupling strengths, and a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for computing ground state energy is derived.

Conclusion: The findings enhance the understanding of quantum Hamiltonians related to the Lee-Yang theorem, allowing for efficient computation of ground state energies.

Abstract: The Lee-Yang theorem and its quantum extensions state that, for a broad class of Hamiltonians on any graph, the partition function's zeros in the complex magnetic field plane lie only on the imaginary axis. For these Hamiltonians, we prove that under a uniform Z-field of any strength h, the ground state has a spectral gap of at least h/4, independent of the system size and of the coupling strengths. The proof uses the zero-freeness of the partition function as given by Asano and Suzuki-Fisher to show exponential decay of the imaginary-time correlations for any product of Z-operators. Our result gives a polynomial-time quantum algorithm for computing the ground state energy of any Lee-Yang Hamiltonian.

</details>


### [43] [Demonstrating Quadratic Monte Carlo Speedup via Quantum Amplitude Estimation: Nuclear Engineering Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10772)
*Jilang Miao,Miaomiao Jin,Akira Sone*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) provides a quadratic speedup over classical Monte Carlo simulations for nuclear engineering applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance the efficiency of Monte Carlo methods in evaluating expectation values in nuclear engineering.

Method: Quantum amplitude estimation was performed using quantum phase estimation (QPE), implemented on a gate-level Qiskit circuit for two examples: fission-neutron yield and U-238 resonance integral, optimizing the simulation to avoid state-preparation bottlenecks.

Result: In both cases, the squared error decreases as $O(1/T^2)$, achieving significant accuracy; for U-238, the result has a relative error of approximately $0.03%$ with 14 phase-estimation qubits.

Conclusion: QAE effectively outperforms classical methods, demonstrating significant accuracy improvements in evaluating complex nuclear engineering parameters.

Abstract: We demonstrate quantum amplitude estimation (QAE) as a route to quadratic speedup for Monte Carlo-type expectation values in nuclear engineering. Using QPE-based QAE, we study two examples: a discrete fission-neutron-yield expectation and a U-238 resonance integral under a $1/E$ slowing-down spectrum. The toy problem is implemented as a gate-level Qiskit circuit, while the resonance-integral example is simulated through an exact eigendecomposition of the Grover operator to avoid state-preparation decomposition bottlenecks. In both cases, the squared error scales as $O(1/T^2)$ with the number of oracle calls $T$, compared with the classical Monte Carlo scaling $O(1/N)$. For the U-238 example, QAE recovers the resonance integral to approximately $0.03%$ relative error with $m=14$ phase-estimation qubits.

</details>


### [44] [Quantum-enhanced physical-layer threat detection in metropolitan-scale fiber networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10799)
*Yung-Cheng Kao,Siddharth Pal,Alex Forencich,Dylan Cirimelli-Low,Chaohan Cui,Jack Postlewaite,Pao-Kang Chen,Nicola Alic,Saikat Guha,Prithwish Basu,Linran Fan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a scalable method for integrating quantum-enhanced security into communication networks, overcoming challenges in current protocols by embedding quantum features in the physical layer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenges of integrating quantum technology into existing communication networks without hindering scalability due to interdependencies between quantum and classical processes.

Method: The authors propose using bright squeezed light to directly embed quantum features with classical information in the same optical field, analyzed via a cumulative sum method for quantum-enhanced threat detection.

Result: The approach was validated through field deployment over a metropolitan-scale fiber network, demonstrating effective network-level security functionalities.

Conclusion: The results provide a practical framework for integrating quantum-enhanced security into large-scale communication infrastructures, ensuring scalability and compatibility with existing architectures.

Abstract: Network security is widely recognized as a key application of quantum technology. However, its large-scale deployment is hindered by the need for tight coordination between fundamentally different quantum and classical processing steps in conventional protocols. This requirement introduces strong cross-layer interdependencies that conflict with the modular, layered architectures enabling scalability in modern communication networks. Here, we present an alternative strategy that confines all quantum interventions to the physical layer and remains transparently compatible with existing network abstractions. This is achieved by directly embedding quantum features and classical information within the same optical field using bright squeezed light. Physical-layer signals are analyzed using a cumulative sum (CUSUM) method to enable quantum-enhanced threat detection. We validate the practicality of this approach through field deployment over a metropolitan-scale fiber network and further demonstrate network-level security functionalities enabled by physical-layer quantum-enhanced thread detection. These results establish a practical, scalable framework for seamlessly integrating quantum-enhanced security into large-scale communication infrastructure.

</details>


### [45] [GroupFunctions.jl: computing individual entries of the irreducible representations of the unitary group U(d)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10867)
*David Amaro-Alcalá,Konrad Szymański*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: GroupFunctions.jl is a Julia library for computing matrix elements of irreducible U(d) representations, offering symbolic and numerical evaluations, specifically Wigner D-functions for SU(2), and various related functionalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to provide an efficient computational tool in Julia for researchers working with group theory and quantum optics, particularly for calculating group functions and representation operators.

Method: The library employs Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns to enumerate the carrier-space basis and offers capabilities to compute matrix elements, representation operators, and facilitate conversions between different quantum states and their mathematical representations.

Result: The library successfully computes the desired matrix elements, representation operators, and allows for input unitary parameterisation, making outputs compatible with Mathematica.

Conclusion: GroupFunctions.jl significantly enhances computational capabilities in the study of U(d) representations and related quantum optics applications, providing a user-friendly interface for researchers.

Abstract: GroupFunctions.jl is a Julia library for computing individual matrix elements of irreducible representations of U(d). These matrix elements, called group functions, can be evaluated symbolically or numerically. For SU(2), they reduce to the Wigner D-functions. The library computes these matrix elements in a carrier-space basis enumerated by Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns. It can also compute entire representation operators, construct input unitaries from parameterisations common in quantum optics, translate Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns into occupation-number kets, and compute the associated Schur functions. Results can be exported in a form compatible with Mathematica.

</details>


### [46] [Driven Quantum Stars as Controlled Primitives for Real-Time Spin Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10899)
*Michael Chertkov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper assesses quantum advantage in spin dynamics against classical substitutes through a novel diagnostic, relating them by a hierarchy of increasingly refined theories based on control parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a clear benchmark for quantum advantage in real-time spin dynamics by comparing quantum models to robust classical alternatives.

Method: A qubit spin model is derived into a spin-Landau--Lifshitz (LL) sector, utilizing graph coordination to organize the quantum sector as controlled corrections; the study involves driven and static spin stars with varying couplings.

Result: Proven exact elimination of leaves in coherent-state return amplitudes, yielding a continuous-time hierarchy structured by the parameter 1/d, which quantitatively characterizes the transition from classical to quantum behavior.

Conclusion: The proposed hierarchy facilitates a physical understanding of dynamical transitions, revealing that each truncation level is an independent theory, ultimately establishing the LL sector as a limit of high coordination.

Abstract: Quantum advantage in real-time spin dynamics should be assessed against the strongest relevant classical substitutes, not merely against the qubit nature of the microscopic system. We develop a physics-based diagnostic for this boundary by reducing a qubit spin model to a spin-Landau--Lifshitz (LL) classical sector and organizing the residual quantum sector as controlled corrections. The control parameter is graph coordination: we study a spin star with \(d\) leaves and \(O(1/d)\) hub--leaf couplings. In its homogeneous form the star benchmarks the transition from LL-substitutable dynamics to genuinely quantum, discrete-sector interference; in its fully driven form, with time-dependent fields and bilinear couplings, it is the basic message-passing primitive for tree and loopy spin structures. For coherent-state return amplitudes we prove exact leaf elimination and derive a continuous-time \(1/d\) hierarchy. L0 is a driven one-spin weak-mean-field theory, while G1 is a Gaussian nonlocal-in-time influence correction coupling leaf two-time kernels to the hub weak two-point function. On bounded finite-time windows away from zeros of the boundary amplitudes, the hierarchy gives \(\log\mathcal A-\log\mathcal A_{\rm L0}=O(1/d)\) and \(\log\mathcal A-\log\mathcal A_{\rm L0}-Δ_{\rm G1}=O(1/d^2)\); numerical tests on fully driven anisotropic ensembles give slopes \(-1.05\) and \(-2.03\). Static, inhomogeneous, aligned, and fully driven stars provide validation rungs, and comparison with a temporal matrix-product influence-matrix baseline delineates complementary regimes. Unlike rank compression on a Trotter grid, the hierarchy is ordered by a physical parameter, formulated in continuous time, and each truncation level is itself a physical theory, with the LL sector as the high-coordination limit.

</details>


### [47] [Randomized Quantum Optimal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10946)
*Leeseok Kim,Francisco Riberi,Kevin Baca,Milad Marvian*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a randomized quantum optimal control (QOC) framework that optimizes ensembles of control functions, achieving faster target accuracy than deterministic methods under similar constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the speed and effectiveness of quantum control techniques by utilizing ensembles of control functions instead of relying solely on deterministic protocols.

Method: A randomized QOC framework optimizes an ensemble of control functions and their probabilities, along with symmetry-based constructions that systematically decrease error.

Result: Benchmarks for CNOT implementation demonstrate that the randomized QOC quadratically reduces error compared to the optimized deterministic approach, with the introduction of randomized GRAPE for control optimization.

Conclusion: The randomized QOC framework provides enhanced performance and robustness, particularly against coherent noise, making it a promising avenue for quantum control applications.

Abstract: Quantum optimal control (QOC) aims to find control functions that optimally steer a quantum system toward a target operation. We introduce a \emph{randomized} QOC framework where optimization is carried over an ensemble of control functions and their probabilities, instead of a single set of functions. Using this framework, we prove that randomized QOC can reach a target accuracy faster than any deterministic protocol under the same resource constraints. We also develop general symmetry-based constructions that convert a given control into an ensemble of controls that can systematically reduce the error. We benchmark these constructions for CNOT implementation and find that the resulting randomized protocol quadratically suppresses the error of the optimized deterministic solution. In addition, we introduce randomized GRAPE, which generalizes GRAPE to directly optimize control ensembles and their associated probabilities. Finally, as a related application, we discuss randomized boundary-pulse constructions {that enhance} robustness against coherent noise.

</details>


### [48] [$p$-Form Gauge Dynamics and Digital Quantum Simulation -- Flux and Cosmological Constant Neutralization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10950)
*Soo-Jong Rey*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a Hamiltonian framework for gauge fields in arbitrary dimensions, detailing definitions of gauge qudits, boundary qudits, and defining operators, and discusses both reduced and unreduced models relevant for understanding phase transitions in quantum systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The development of a Hamiltonian framework for ${	extbf Z}_k$ $p$-form gauge fields on cell complexes aims to address complex structures in quantum error correction and phase transitions in quantum systems.

Method: The framework includes defining gauge qudits through cell structures, analyzing magnetic aspects with boundary operators, and studying special cases like $p=2, k=2$ with empirical evaluation using exact diagonalization on small tori.

Result: Findings reveal significant flux area loss during tube-cap quenches and establish a dynamical crossover point for the system, linking higher-form Hamiltonians and coding schemes.

Conclusion: This research integrates higher-form gauge theories with quantum coding frameworks, providing insights into cosmological constants and specific discharge processes.

Abstract: I develop a Hamiltonian framework for ${\mathbb Z}_k$ $p$-form gauge fields on arbitrary oriented cell complexes in arbitrary dimensions. Gauge qudits are defined by $p$-cells, charged boundary qudits by $(p-1)$-cells, Gauss-law generators by boundary map $\partial_p$, and magnetic checks by $\partial_{p+1}$. The same cellular structure produces local dressed Wilson operators, and at $k=2$ a Calderbank-Shor-Steane check complex relevant to quantum error correction. I then specialize to $p=2, k=2$, where the magnetic 3-cell term is absent and the one-form Gauss-law can be solved exactly. The physical Hilbert space is parameterized by plaquette electric-flux variables, while the link configuration is reconstructed as the dynamical boundary of the evolving flux domains. The reduced Hamiltonian is an Ising-type plaquette model, where its local transverse-field term is the physical image of the boundary-dressed Wilson operator $σ_p^z\prod_{\ell\in\partial p}τ_\ell^z$. A tube-cap quench compares two initial flux fillings with the same initial boundary loops. Exact diagonalization on $4\times4$, $6\times4$, and $5\times5$ tori finds that the cap loses $20$-$37\%$ of its occupied-flux area, while the tube remains nearly pinned. A finite-size scaling locates a dynamical crossover of tension-to-density ratio near $(m/\varepsilon_E)_c\simeq1.89$. The unreduced plaquette-plus-link encoding provides local Gauss-law checks and a direct digital implementation, while the reduced plaquette-only Hamiltonian supplies the exact benchmark. The result places the specific top-form discharge and the cosmological constant neutralization calculation inside a general higher-form Hamiltonian and coding framework.

</details>


### [49] [The quantum coherence as a phase property of the wave function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10958)
*Ivan Georgiev Koprinkov*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces the concept of quantum coherence through nonadiabatic dressed states influenced by an external electromagnetic field, highlighting two distinct types of phase correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To elucidate the phase properties of quantum coherence, particularly focusing on the wave function and its behaviors under specific interactions.

Method: The study investigates nonadiabatic dressed states formed through the interaction between a quantum system and a coherent electromagnetic field.

Result: The research identifies two types of phase correlations: a rapidly changing correlation between real and virtual components, and a stationary correlation among different virtual components.

Conclusion: The findings provide new insights into the quantum coherence of multilevel systems and their phase correlations, which may enhance the understanding of quantum mechanics.

Abstract: The quantum coherence as a phase property of the most fundamental quantum mechanical entity, the wave function, is explained for the first time. Phase-sensitive nonadiabatic dressed states, arising from the interaction of a quantum system and a coherent external electromagnetic field, are used in these considerations. Two types of phase correlations in the multilevel phase-sensitive nonadiabatic dressed states are found: a rapidly changing phase correlation between the real and the virtual components and a stationary phase correlation between different virtual components of these states.

</details>


### [50] [Overcoming Fourier Locking in Quantum Data Re-uploading Classifiers via Spectral Homotopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11013)
*Spencer Topel*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Data re-uploading parameterized quantum circuits face optimization challenges due to a structural issue known as Fourier locking (FL), which causes loss landscapes that impede effective training. The paper identifies FL and proposes a frequency-staged homotopy protocol to improve optimization outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the optimization of DRU-PQCs, which struggle with oscillatory loss landscapes attributed to Fourier locking, leading to ineffective training despite their universal approximator capabilities.

Method: The study employs two Fisher diagnostics to examine the phenomenon of Fourier locking in DRU-PQCs and implements a frequency-staged homotopy protocol that adjusts the target frequency during training to enhance the loss landscape.

Result: Experiments demonstrated that escaping circuits significantly improved their performance metrics, including an increase in the effective frequency content ($F_x$) and a tripling of the escape rate when frequency pacing was applied.

Conclusion: Fourier locking is fundamentally a frequency-alignment issue; therefore, applying frequency pacing is an effective strategy to mitigate this problem and aid the optimization of DRU-PQCs.

Abstract: Data re-uploading parameterized quantum circuits (DRU-PQCs) are universal function approximators, yet their expressivity produces oscillatory, non-convex loss landscapes that resist gradient-based optimization. We show that the primary optimization bottleneck in DRU-PQCs is not insufficient capacity but a structural failure mode we term Fourier locking (FL): because encoding weights and entangling layers are nonlinearly coupled, random initialization on high-frequency targets collapses the encoding parameters into spurious local minima. Two Fisher diagnostics characterize FL. The input-space quantum Fisher information $F_x$ measures the effective frequency content of the encoded state; the Fisher discriminant ratio of the measured features measures their alignment with the class labels. In two independent 50-seed experiments, the locking is literal: trapped circuits hold $F_x$ frozen for the entire run, while escaping circuits migrate their frequency content (direct training: $r_{pb} = -0.48$; curriculum: $d = 1.34$; both $p < 0.001$). The replicated signature is this spectral mobility, not any endpoint value of $F_x$, and trapped circuits retain a fully non-degenerate parameter-space QFIM ($r_{pb} \approx 0$): the failure is spectral misalignment of a responsive state, not a loss of geometric sensitivity. A frequency-staged homotopy protocol that paces the target frequency ($f: 1.0 \to 3.0$) convexifies the early loss landscape; escaping circuits raise $F_x$ in step with the curriculum, and the escape rate triples (18% vs. 6%). Fourier locking is a frequency-alignment problem, and its remedy is frequency pacing.

</details>


### [51] [Many-Body Physics with Rydberg Atoms: Quantum Simulation and Non-equilibrium Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11038)
*Zhengyang Bai,Cheng Chen,Fan Yang,Weibin Li*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This review highlights recent advancements in Rydberg atom research, focusing on their dipole-dipole interactions, many-body physics, and implications for quantum technologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore collective effects and many-body dynamics in Rydberg atoms, which have significant implications for quantum simulations and non-equilibrium physics.

Method: The review discusses theoretical underpinnings and experimental techniques for manipulating Rydberg atomic ensembles, highlighting both theoretical insights and experimental breakthroughs.

Result: Recent experiments have demonstrated novel many-body phases and non-equilibrium dynamics in systems of Rydberg atoms, showcasing their collective effects and potential applications.

Conclusion: The work synthesizes theoretical and experimental perspectives, indicating the transformative potential of Rydberg atoms in the future of quantum technology.

Abstract: Rydberg atoms, characterized by their strong and long-range dipole-dipole interactions, provide a versatile platform for exploring intriguing collective and many-body effects. Recently, the experimental realization of these effects in dense ensembles and reconfigurable atomic arrays has attracted significant interest, particularly for applications in quantum simulations and non-equilibrium physics. This review focuses on such recent development, discussing the theoretical foundations of the interactions between Rydberg atoms and the ensuing many-body physics, while providing a critical survey of experimental techniques for their precise manipulation and observation. We further discuss recent breakthroughs in leveraging Rydberg collective effects to probe novel many-body phases and non-equilibrium dynamics of these systems. By synthesizing theoretical insights with experimental milestones, we provide a comprehensive perspective on this rapidly evolving field and its transformative potential for future quantum technologies.

</details>


### [52] [When cheap gradients fail: the measurement cost of attacking quantum classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11095)
*Bacui Li,Chandra Thapa,Tansu Alpcan,Udaya Parampalli*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: Finite quantum measurement statistics provide a defense mechanism against adversarial attacks on variational quantum classifiers, leading to increased costs for attackers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the vulnerability of machine learning classifiers, especially variational quantum classifiers, to adversarial perturbations.

Method: The authors analyze the effects of shot noise on gradient-based attacks, showing that attackers face a considerable measurement cost that scales with input dimension.

Result: Experiments and simulations demonstrate that the measurement cost for attackers grows significantly with input dimensions, resulting in a cost ratio that diverges as the model scales.

Conclusion: The defense is effective particularly when the forward map is challenging for classical simulation, making it costly for attackers to execute successful gradient-based attacks.

Abstract: Adversarial perturbations threaten machine learning classifiers, including variational quantum classifiers. We show that finite quantum measurement statistics (shot noise) act as a built-in defense against gradient-based test-time attacks whose cost scales unfavorably for the attacker. Because every gradient component must be inferred from repeated circuit executions under any unbiased gradient-estimation rule, white-box extraction consumes a dimension-dependent measurement budget that measurement grouping cannot remove in expressive circuits. Under stated assumptions, single-step attacks need at least quadratically many shots in the input dimension $d$, growing as $d^{5/2}$ under norm-concentration scaling, with a sufficient-budget analysis for iterative attacks via stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics. Simulations up to 784 input dimensions validate the law: the realized total budget is the $d^{5/2}$ geometric floor for plateau-mitigated models and grows as $d^{3.00}$ for the tested deep circuits, whose gradient norms decay with dimension absent barren-plateau mitigation; folding the measured gradient norm back in recovers the parameter-free $d^{3/2}$ shot-noise geometry. Against a matched classical baseline whose attack overhead is dimension-independent (the cheap-gradient principle of automatic differentiation), the quantum gradient cost ratio grows empirically as $d^{3.00}$, so the attacker's relative cost diverges as the model scales. Experiments on a 156-qubit IBM processor (ibm_boston, 4-qubit circuits, $d=12$) reproduce the effect: at matched budgets the device attack tracks the ideal within a few percent, with the high-shot gradient faithful to the exact one. The defense operates precisely when the forward map is classically hard to simulate: only then is a white-box attacker denied the simulate-and-backpropagate shortcut and must pay the measurement cost we quantify.

</details>


### [53] [Decoherence of a quantum magneto-oscillator: Effect of field-bath interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11137)
*Koushik Mandal,Suraka Bhattacharjee,Supurna Sinha*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study examines the quantum dissipative dynamics of a charged magneto-oscillator linked to a heat bath, emphasizing the impact of field-bath interaction on decoherence and classical transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how field-bath coupling influences the quantum to classical transition and the decoherence dynamics in charged systems.

Method: The problem is analyzed using the generalized quantum Langevin equation, leading to a non-Markovian master equation to investigate the impact of field-bath coupling on the reduced density matrix.

Result: The study finds that field-bath coupling enhances the decoherence rate by modifying the dissipation response of the bath, affecting correlation properties like position-autocorrelation and velocity-autocorrelation.

Conclusion: The findings highlight the significance of field-bath interactions in quantum systems and suggest paths for experimental verification, contributing to advancements in quantum technologies.

Abstract: We investigate the quantum dissipative dynamics of a charged magneto-oscillator(CMO) coupled to a heat bath of harmonic oscillators in the presence of an additional field-bath interaction. The problem is formulated within the framework of the generalized quantum Langevin equation, where the external confining potential modifies the random force correlations and the memory kernel of the environment, and the effective spring constant. Using the corresponding non-Markovian master equation, we examine the influence of the field-bath coupling on the temporal decay of the reduced density matrix of the system. We show that the additional interaction leads to an enhancement of the decoherence rate by altering the dissipative response of the bath. Thus our results shed light on the role of field-bath coupling in the quantum to classical transition of a CMO. The resulting fluctuation-dissipation relation is analyzed, and the position autocorrelation, position-velocity correlation and velocity autocorrelation are obtained explicitly. The dependence of these experimentally accessible quantities on the field-bath coupling parameter is discussed. We outline an experimental proposal for testing our theoretical predictions. The study sheds light on reservoir engineering which is central to quantum technologies.

</details>


### [54] [Lie-Algebraic Subspace Quantization for Zero-Shot Quantum Learning and Barren-Plateau Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11174)
*Yuhan Yao,Yoshihiko Hasegawa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper addresses the barren plateau phenomenon in parameterized quantum circuits by proposing a classical-to-quantum parameter transfer method and model merging without quantum optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The barren plateau phenomenon limits the scalability of parameterized quantum circuits, necessitating effective parameter transfer methods to enhance performance.

Method: An analytical framework is introduced for zero-shot classical-to-quantum parameter transfer, utilizing techniques like Stiefel subspace selection and nearest-unitary polar projection to convert classical neural-network weights into quantum evolutions.

Result: The proposed method achieves a Hellinger fidelity of 0.987 on IBM's ibm_kobe with k=8, demonstrating effective parameter transfer and error bounds derived from the Subspace Quantization Theorem.

Conclusion: The framework mitigates issues related to initialization and provides a reliable warm-start initialization, proving beneficial for practical implementations of PQCs.

Abstract: The barren plateau phenomenon severely limits the scalability of parameterized quantum circuits (PQCs). We present an analytical framework for zero-shot classical-to-quantum parameter transfer and manifold-based model merging without quantum-side optimization. Our parameter transfer map converts classical neural-network weights into low-dimensional quantum evolutions using Stiefel subspace selection, nearest-unitary polar projection, and logarithmic generator extraction. We prove a Subspace Quantization Theorem that bounds the reconstruction error by geometric truncation and exact non-unitarity, with the latter becoming second order in the near-unitary regime. Identity-centered architectures naturally produce generators near the identity, avoiding logarithm branch-cut singularities and mitigating initialization-time gradient concentration. We further derive an explicit error bound for manifold-based model merging and show that the same construction provides a warm-start initialization whose active dynamics remain confined to a k-dimensional subspace. Experiments on IBM ibm_kobe demonstrate Hellinger fidelity of 0.987 at k=8, while subspace gradients remain resolvable up to 128 physical qubits.

</details>


### [55] [Dynamics of two interacting dipolar two-level systems in a multi-mode electromagnetic cavity: sudden death and revival of the concurrence within the Born-Markov approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11201)
*Loann Quien,Naomichi Hatano*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper studies the dynamics of dipolar two-level systems (qubits) interacting with a quantized magnetic field, proving equivalences and deriving density matrices, concurrence, and temporal behaviors via Born-Markov approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the interaction dynamics of dipolar two-level systems in a thermal environment and derive key properties affecting their coherence and entanglement.

Method: The study involves proving equivalences between spin interactions and two-level atom interactions using the Born-Markov approximation, analyzing correlations, and deriving density matrices for two interacting spins.

Result: The paper provides explicit expressions for the density matrix, decay times, and stationary concurrence, with numerical computations showing phenomena like sudden death and revival of entanglement.

Conclusion: The findings highlight the significant role of the singlet state and provide equations for XXZ coupling, demonstrating valuable insights into quantum system dynamics in thermal environments.

Abstract: Interacting dipolar two-level systems form a special class of qubits that interact with a cavity in a particular way. We first prove that the Markovian dynamics of one 1/2-spin in interaction with a quantised magnetic field from a multi-mode cavity at thermal equilibrium is equivalent to a two-level atom interacting in the dipole approximation with the electric field of the cavity. We then use the Born-Markov approximation to study the dynamics of two spins interacting through the antiferromagnetic Heisenberg coupling in the same environment. We find the exact expression of the density matrix of the system, with the off-diagonal coherence decay time and spin relaxation time. The concurrence for the stationary state is explicitly derived for any kind of initial state and the role of the singlet state is brought to light. The temporal evolution of the concurrence is numerically computed for different initial states, the phenomenon of sudden death and revival is observed even within the Born-Markov approximation, and an analysis of its behaviour is conducted for Werner states. We finally derive the equations and the stationary concurrence for the XXZ coupling.

</details>


### [56] [Unique temporal scaling dimension for quantum criticality in open systems weakly coupled to environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11206)
*Fan Zhong*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel approach to quantum phase transitions in open systems, focusing on a new temporal renormalization-group eigenvalue that alters critical exponents and allows the formulation of a general scaling theory, particularly addressing Kibble-Zurek scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding quantum phase transitions in open systems is essential for advancing fields like condensed matter physics and quantum computing.

Method: The paper proposes a temporal renormalization-group eigenvalue specifically for quantum criticality in weakly coupled open systems, leading to a general scaling theory.

Result: The study shows that critical exponents of time-related quantities change nonperturbatively with weak coupling, except for an Ohmic bath, thereby enhancing the understanding of critical properties in these systems.

Conclusion: This work lays a foundation for future investigations into quantum phase transitions in open systems and provides insights into the critical scaling behavior, especially concerning Kibble-Zurek dynamics.

Abstract: Probing, understanding, predicting, and controlling the real-time dynamics of quantum phase transitions in open systems are of pivotal importance to modern condensed matter physics, statistical physics, and quantum computing, among others. Here it is argued that a distinct temporal renormalization-group eigenvalue is needed for quantum criticality in open systems weakly coupled to their finite-temperature environment. This new physics enables the formulation of a general scaling theory that can accurately account for the critical properties including the specific Kibble-Zurek scaling in such open quantum systems. Remarkably, the critical exponents of time-related quantities are altered nonperturbatively regardless of how weak the coupling is, except for an Ohmic bath. Perspectives for future study are also discussed.

</details>


### [57] [Distributed Semantics for Distributed Quantum Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11216)
*Jun Inoue*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a quantum process calculus that enables independent analysis of quantum processes through spatial compositionality, using Deutsch-Hayden descriptors for modular state representation, while still managing entanglement and information flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve compositional reasoning in quantum systems, which traditional quantum process calculi have struggled to deliver without losing information about entanglement.

Method: The authors utilize Deutsch-Hayden descriptors to model quantum states, allowing for arbitrary splitting and merging of qubits, leading to localized views of system states and a novel process calculus.

Result: The proposed calculus demonstrates spatial compositionality and defines process equivalence, validated through a simplified bisimulation soundness proof and successful modeling of an open system in the BB84 key distribution protocol.

Conclusion: Deutsch-Hayden descriptors effectively track qubit movement, facilitating independent analysis of processes while relying on density matrices for information flow reasoning.

Abstract: We present a quantum process calculus that can split the system state along process boundaries and follow the evolution of each process in isolation, without losing information about the joint state-a property we call spatial compositionality. Compositionality is the key to reasoning about any complex system, yet quantum process calculi have struggled to provide its spatial kind, which would enable analyzing a system one process at a time. Many a quantum process calculi have been proposed, but they invariably rely on a global state representation based on state vectors or density matrices, with no known way to split them without losing information about entanglement. We propose to model quantum states with Deutsch-Hayden descriptors instead, which provide a modular representation of qubit states and their evolution. We adapt these descriptors to allow arbitrary splitting and merging of the store of qubits, leading to an unusual process calculus in which qubit transfer messages carry the actual state of the qubit, where existing calculi transfer only a reference. The calculus gives localized views of system state visible to each process, which can be assembled back together into the joint state. We define a notion of process equivalence with extensive justification grounded in physics and show a bisimulation whose soundness proof is simplified by spatial compositionality. The calculus can model open systems entangled with external processes, and we demonstrate this capability on a fragment of the BB84 key distribution protocol. This exercise shows that Deutsch-Hayden descriptors can successfully track qubit movements across process and system boundaries, though it needs help from density matrices to reason about information flow.

</details>


### [58] [Quantum Multiscale Modeling: A Hierarchy of Algorithms for Complex Chemical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11217)
*Seenivasan Hariharan,Kareljan Schoutens,Sachin Kinge,Lucas Visscher*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores the multiscale modeling of chemical systems through quantum algorithms, highlighting the need for coherent interplay between different scales and addressing unresolved questions about state preparation and information loss during inter-scale transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a systematic framework for composing quantum algorithms that function across electronic, atomistic, mesoscopic, and continuum scales in chemical modeling.

Method: The authors analyze various quantum algorithms and their applicability to specific scales, focusing on conditions necessary for maintaining quantum advantages while identifying unresolved questions regarding the inter-scale transfer of information.

Result: They correlate quantum algorithms with respective modeling regimes but emphasize that speedups are conditional on factors such as state preparation and memory architecture.

Conclusion: The study presents a roadmap for achieving multiscale quantum advantages, suggesting that effective information transfer between algorithmic layers is crucial for overall performance, rather than just isolated scale efficiency.

Abstract: Multiscale modeling of complex chemical systems requires algorithms that operate coherently across electronic, atomistic, mesoscopic, and continuum scales. While quantum algorithms have been proposed for each regime, no systematic framework exists to compose them across scale boundaries. Here, we identify the conditions under which fault-tolerant quantum algorithms might preserve scale-specific quantum advantages. We map quantum phase estimation, Hamiltonian simulation with Gibbs state preparation, quantum random walks, and quantum partial differential equation solvers onto electronic structure, molecular dynamics, mesoscopic kinetics, and continuum reactor physics, respectively. Crucially, these correspondences do not imply unconditional end-to-end quantum advantage; speedups depend heavily on state preparation, memory architectures, matrix conditioning, and classical readout costs. Six unresolved questions define this composition problem, illustrated via a quantum hierarchy for \ce{CO} oxidation over \ce{Pt(111)}. We propose viewing inter-scale transfer as a quantum channel composition problem at the interface of algorithm design and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, and ask whether information loss at scale boundaries is intrinsic to multiscale modeling or merely a consequence of lossy classical transduction between algorithmic layers. The resulting roadmap suggests that multiscale quantum advantage is governed primarily by the structure of information transfer between algorithmic layers, rather than by performance at individual scales alone.

</details>


### [59] [Which Classicality? Incidence, Simplex, and Product-Rule Tests in Finite Quantum Logics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11234)
*Karl Svozil*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper explores the classical and nonclassical nature of finite quantum-logical structures, focusing on various tests, specifically highlighting when GHZ measurements become nonclassical based on specific conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the conditions under which quantum-logical structures manifest classical or nonclassical behaviors through different types of tests.

Method: The study employs incidence tests, simplex-embedding tests, and operator-functional tests, particularly analyzing the conditions of GHZ joint measurements and computing state-depolarizing thresholds for selected fragments.

Result: It was found that GHZ measurements are Boolean in nature and transition to a nonclassical state under the requirement of certain local factor assignments and product preservation. Also, state-depolarizing thresholds were computed, revealing properties linked to fragments and noise models.

Conclusion: The results indicate that the characteristics of quantum-logical fragments and noise are properties of the fragments themselves rather than universal features derived from the underlying structures.

Abstract: Finite quantum-logical constructions can appear classical or nonclassical depending on which structure is retained. We distinguish incidence tests based on valuations, colorings, and partition representations; simplex-embedding tests for specified prepare-and-measure fragments; and operator-functional tests imposing spectral and product rules. We show that the collective GHZ joint measurement is a single Boolean context and becomes nonclassical only when a common assignment of local factors, together with product preservation, is required. For selected labelled ray fragments, we compute state-depolarizing thresholds for a restricted projector-cone factorization and for a specified vector-generated operational closure, separating exact primal--dual certificates from numerical estimates. These values are properties of the stated fragments and noise model, not invariants of the underlying hypergraphs or a universal ordering of contextuality.

</details>


### [60] [Asymmetry-aided measurement-based quantum repeaters and distributed quantum computing with a decoder-free client](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11238)
*Wooyeong Song,Sungyeon Kook,Wonhyuk Lee,IlKwon Sohn*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores the integration of measurement-based quantum repeaters with quantum computation, presenting a more efficient approach using a Bacon-Shor subsystem code for qubit transmission over long distances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance the efficiency of transmitting logical qubits across lossy optical links in distributed quantum computing.

Method: The authors leverage a rectangular Bacon-Shor subsystem code that recognizes structural asymmetries in loss concentrated on transmission, enabling more effective qubit transmission than traditional methods.

Result: The proposed code demonstrates a significant efficiency improvement in qubit transmission, achieving a cost-optimal repeater density that is about an order of magnitude lower than previous benchmarks.

Conclusion: The approach allows for effective integration of transmission and computation in distributed quantum setups, facilitating decoder-free client operations.

Abstract: Distributed quantum computation needs to move logical qubits across lossy optical links, yet this transmission layer is usually designed separately from the computation it serves. We treat the two together by recognizing that a measurement-based quantum repeater is a two-dimensional code foliated along the transmission axis, so that the dominant channel loss is concentrated on the transmitted sector while the locally measured qubits are largely spared. Matching a code's distance to this structural asymmetry, we show that a rectangular Bacon-Shor subsystem code transmits a logical qubit markedly more efficiently than transmission-unaware encodings. Over continental distances, its cost-optimal repeater density is about an order of magnitude lower than that of a recent $[[48,6,8]]$ benchmark at comparable transmission rate, and roughly half that of a symmetric code of equal size. Moreover, we extend the framework to a central-to-client round trip in which a code-level, distance-preserving code switch joins the transmission legs to the client's computation, and joint decoding of the heterogeneous syndrome record at the central node lets distributed quantum computation proceed with a decoder-free client.

</details>


### [61] [Deployment of Entanglement-Based QKD in Financial Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11252)
*Mirela Selimović,Roman Solar,Jonathan Gruner,Sebastian Mair,Mario Wenzl,Thomas Heine,Matej Pivoluska,Rupert Ursin,Sebastian Philipp Neumann*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study demonstrates the successful implementation of entanglement-based quantum key distribution (eQKD) in a financial setting, achieving high security and operational efficiency over a 22 km fiber link.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the need for secure communication in financial infrastructures, utilizing quantum technology to enhance security protocols against potential cyber threats.

Method: A fully automated eQKD system using polarization entanglement was operated over a 22 km fiber link, continuously generating secure keys for VPN tunnels with active polarization control and efficient timing synchronization.

Result: The system maintained a total up-time of 93.7%, generated keys at 63.8 kb/s, kept the quantum bit error rate below 2% for most of the time, and achieved sub-300 ps timing precision.

Conclusion: The successful integration of eQKD into financial infrastructure demonstrates its potential for enhancing security in critical applications without the need for supplemental high-precision equipment.

Abstract: We demonstrate the feasibility of entanglement-based quantum key distribution (eQKD) in high-security financial infrastructure over a 22 km fiber link with 8 dB loss between two data centers using polarization entanglement. The fully automated system continuously generated secure keys for four months at an average rate of 63.8 kb/s, which were stored into a key management system and consumed to establish a VPN tunnel. The setup achieved 93.7% total up-time, with no downtime caused by the quantum optical components. Active polarization control kept the quantum bit error rate below 2% for 97.4% of the time and timing synchronization based on the entangled photon pairs' intrinsic temporal correlations achieved sub-300 ps precision. Our standalone system requires neither polarized guide lasers nor external high-precision time references. These results show practical integration of eQKD into operational financial infrastructure.

</details>


### [62] [Fixed-Protocol Amortized MPS Tomography with Conformalized Predictive Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11273)
*Jian Xu,Delu Zeng,John Paisley,Qibin Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenges of quantum state tomography, proposing a new estimator that leverages informative measurement designs to enhance fidelity with fewer samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum state tomography suffers from sample limitations; the authors aim to improve measurement efficiency by developing a model that utilizes measurements in a meaningful way.

Method: The authors propose two approaches: Approach A uses a generative prior for matrix-product states with posterior inference based on measurements, while Approach B introduces a fixed-protocol amortized matrix-product-state estimator that leverages informative local measurement strategies.

Result: The new method (Approach B) achieves high fidelity (approximately 0.95), significantly improving upon prior estimates and demonstrating robust performance as the system size increases.

Conclusion: By focusing on informative measurement designs, the proposed method enhances the accuracy of quantum state estimation and is validated on IBM hardware.

Abstract: Quantum state tomography is sample-starved, and the states one prepares live on a narrow, learnable manifold. A $k{=}0$ prior-only control shows that on concentrated families a prior estimate is already near-optimal, so ``high fidelity at few measurements'' can be family memorization rather than tomography; genuine measurement-efficiency needs a model that conditions on the measurements and demonstrably uses them. On a shared matrix-product-state (MPS) core parameterization we study two routes. Approach~A learns a generative prior over MPS cores with measurement-guided posterior inference (gold-standard-validated, but whose few-measurement accuracy the control shows is largely the prior). Approach~B, our main proposal, is a \emph{fixed-protocol amortized} MPS estimator trained once with a gauge-invariant fidelity loss; we deliberately do not rest it on a permutation-invariant set encoder (a plain MLP matches it). The decisive lever is the measurement design: motivated by the fact that local reduced density matrices determine a $χ$-MPS, conditioning on an \emph{informative local} Pauli set rather than random strings turns a modest, memorization-prone estimator into a high-fidelity one ($\approx\!0.95$, up to $+0.59$ over prior-only, decisively passing a shuffled-measurement control). A dropout ensemble, conformally recalibrated, gives $\approx\!90\%$-coverage intervals -- including for observables never measured, where a shot-based interval does not exist.
